<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Liszt_1858.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Liszt_1858.jpg/170px-Liszt_1858.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="344" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Liszt_1858.jpg/255px-Liszt_1858.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Liszt_1858.jpg/340px-Liszt_1858.jpg 2x" data-file-width="667" data-file-height="1351" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Franz_Liszt" title="Franz Liszt">Franz Liszt</a>, one of the greatest pianists of all time; a renowned composer and conductor</figcaption></figure>
<p>Folk music is a prominent part of the national identity and has been significant in former country parts that belong—since the 1920 Treaty of Trianon—to neighbouring countries such as Romania, Slovakia, Poland and especially in southern Slovakia and Transylvania. After the establishment of a music academy led by Liszt and <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Erkel" title="Ferenc Erkel">Ferenc Erkel</a>, Hungary produced an important number of art musicians:
</p>
<ul><li>Pianists: Ernő von Dohnányi, <a href="/wiki/Ervin_Nyiregyh%C3%A1zi" title="Ervin Nyiregyházi">Ervin Nyiregyházi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andor_F%C3%B6ldes" title="Andor Földes">Andor Földes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tam%C3%A1s_V%C3%A1s%C3%A1ry" title="Tamás Vásáry">Tamás Vásáry</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gy%C3%B6rgy_S%C3%A1ndor" title="György Sándor">György Sándor</a>, <a href="/wiki/G%C3%A9za_Anda" title="Géza Anda">Géza Anda</a>, <a href="/wiki/Annie_Fischer" title="Annie Fischer">Annie Fischer</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gy%C3%B6rgy_Cziffra" title="György Cziffra">György Cziffra</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Kilenyi_Jr." title="Edward Kilenyi Jr.">Edward Kilényi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Balint_Vazsonyi" title="Balint Vazsonyi">Bálint Vázsonyi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A1s_Schiff" title="András Schiff">András Schiff</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zolt%C3%A1n_Kocsis" title="Zoltán Kocsis">Zoltán Kocsis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dezs%C5%91_R%C3%A1nki" title="Dezső Ránki">Dezső Ránki</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jen%C5%91_Jand%C3%B3" title="Jenő Jandó">Jenő Jandó</a> and others.</li>
<li>Violinists: <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Joachim" title="Joseph Joachim">Joseph Joachim</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leopold_Auer" title="Leopold Auer">Leopold Auer</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jen%C5%91_Hubay" title="Jenő Hubay">Jenő Hubay</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jelly_d%27Ar%C3%A1nyi" title="Jelly d&#39;Arányi">Jelly d'Arányi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Szigeti" title="Joseph Szigeti">Joseph Szigeti</a>, <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A1ndor_V%C3%A9gh" title="Sándor Végh">Sándor Végh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Emil_Telmanyi" class="mw-redirect" title="Emil Telmanyi">Emil Telmanyi</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Zathurecky&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Zathurecky (page does not exist)">Ede Zathurecky</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zsigmondy" title="Zsigmondy">Zsigmondy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Franz_von_Vecsey" title="Franz von Vecsey">Franz von Vecsey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zolt%C3%A1n_Sz%C3%A9kely" title="Zoltán Székely">Zoltán Székely</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tibor_Varga_(violinist)" title="Tibor Varga (violinist)">Tibor Varga</a> and newcomers <a href="/wiki/Antal_Szalai" class="mw-redirect" title="Antal Szalai">Antal Szalai</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vilmos_Szabadi" title="Vilmos Szabadi">Vilmos Szabadi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Krist%C3%B3f_Bar%C3%A1ti" title="Kristóf Baráti">Kristóf Baráti</a> and others.</li>
<li>Opera singers: <a href="/wiki/Astrid_Varnay" title="Astrid Varnay">Astrid Varnay</a>, <a href="/wiki/J%C3%B3zsef_Sim%C3%A1ndy" title="József Simándy">József Simándy</a>, <a href="/wiki/J%C3%BAlia_V%C3%A1rady" title="Júlia Várady">Júlia Várady</a>, <a href="/wiki/Julia_Hamari" title="Julia Hamari">Júlia Hamari</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Kolos_Kov%C3%A1ts&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Kolos Kováts (page does not exist)">Kolos Kováts</a> (Bluebeard in Bartók's Bluebeard)</li>
<li>Conductors: <a href="/wiki/Eugene_Ormandy" title="Eugene Ormandy">Eugene Ormandy</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_Szell" title="George Szell">George Szell</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antal_Dor%C3%A1ti" title="Antal Doráti">Antal Doráti</a>, <a href="/wiki/J%C3%A1nos_Ferencsik" title="János Ferencsik">János Ferencsik</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fritz_Reiner" title="Fritz Reiner">Fritz Reiner</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sir_Georg_Solti" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Georg Solti">sir Georg Solti</a>, <a href="/wiki/Istv%C3%A1n_Kert%C3%A9sz_(conductor)" title="István Kertész (conductor)">István Kertész</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Fricsay" title="Ferenc Fricsay">Ferenc Fricsay</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zolt%C3%A1n_Rozsnyai" title="Zoltán Rozsnyai">Zoltán Rozsnyai</a>, <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A1ndor_V%C3%A9gh" title="Sándor Végh">Sándor Végh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arpad_Joo" class="mw-redirect" title="Arpad Joo">Árpád Joó</a>, <a href="/wiki/%C3%81d%C3%A1m_Fischer" title="Ádám Fischer">Ádám Fischer</a>, <a href="/wiki/Iv%C3%A1n_Fischer" title="Iván Fischer">Iván Fischer</a>, <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A9ter_E%C3%B6tv%C3%B6s" title="Péter Eötvös">Péter Eötvös</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zolt%C3%A1n_Kocsis" title="Zoltán Kocsis">Zoltán Kocsis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tam%C3%A1s_V%C3%A1s%C3%A1ry" title="Tamás Vásáry">Tamás Vásáry</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Varga" title="Gilbert Varga">Gilbert Varga</a> and others</li>
<li>String Quartets: <a href="/wiki/Budapest_String_Quartet" title="Budapest String Quartet">Budapest Quartet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_Quartet" title="Hungarian Quartet">Hungarian Quartet</a>, <a href="/wiki/V%C3%A9gh_Quartet" title="Végh Quartet">Végh Quartet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tak%C3%A1cs_Quartet" title="Takács Quartet">Takács Quartet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kod%C3%A1ly_Quartet" title="Kodály Quartet">Kodály Quartet</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%89der_Quartet&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Éder Quartet (page does not exist)">Éder Quartet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Festetics_String_Quartet" title="Festetics String Quartet">Festetics Quartet</a>,</li></ul>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg/170px-Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="157" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg/255px-Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg/340px-Bart%C3%B3k_B%C3%A9la_1927.jpg 2x" data-file-width="411" data-file-height="379" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9la_Bart%C3%B3k" title="Béla Bartók">Béla Bartók</a>, a composer of great influence in the early 20th century; one of the founders of <a href="/wiki/Ethnomusicology" title="Ethnomusicology">ethnomusicology</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Broughton claims that Hungary's "infectious sound has been surprisingly influential on neighboring countries (thanks perhaps to the common Austro-Hungarian history) and it's not uncommon to hear Hungarian-sounding tunes in Romania, Slovakia and Poland".<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> It is also strong in the <a href="/wiki/Szabolcs-Szatm%C3%A1r-Bereg_County" title="Szabolcs-Szatmár-Bereg County">Szabolcs-Szatmár</a> area and in the southwest part of Transdanubia, near the border with Croatia. The <a href="/wiki/Bus%C3%B3j%C3%A1r%C3%A1s" title="Busójárás">Busójárás</a> carnival in Mohács is a major Hungarian folk music event, formerly featuring the long-established and well-regarded <a href="/wiki/Bogyiszl%C3%B3_Orchestra" title="Bogyiszló Orchestra">Bogyiszló Orchestra</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Broughton_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Broughton-242">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hungarian classical music has long been an "experiment, made from Hungarian antecedents and on Hungarian soil, to create a conscious musical culture [using the] musical world of the folk song".<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> Although the Hungarian upper class has long had cultural and political connections with the rest of Europe, leading to an influx of European musical ideas, the rural peasants maintained their own traditions such that by the end of the 19th-century Hungarian composers could draw on rural peasant music to (re)create a Hungarian classical style.<sup id="cite_ref-Szabolcsi_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Szabolcsi-244">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> For example, Bartók collected folk songs from across Central and Eastern Europe, including Romania and Slovakia, while Kodály was more interested in creating a distinctively Hungarian musical style.
</p><p>During the era of communist rule in Hungary (1944–1989), a Song Committee scoured and censored popular music for traces of subversion and ideological impurity. Since then, however, the Hungarian music industry has begun to recover, producing successful performers in the fields of <a href="/wiki/Jazz" title="Jazz">jazz</a> such as trumpeter <a href="/wiki/Rudolf_Tomsits" title="Rudolf Tomsits">Rudolf Tomsits</a>, pianist-composer <a href="/wiki/K%C3%A1roly_Binder" title="Károly Binder">Károly Binder</a> and, in a modernised form of Hungarian folk, <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Seb%C5%91" title="Ferenc Sebő">Ferenc Sebő</a> and <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1rta_Sebesty%C3%A9n" title="Márta Sebestyén">Márta Sebestyén</a>. The three giants of Hungarian rock, <a href="/wiki/Ill%C3%A9s_(band)" title="Illés (band)">Illés</a>, <a href="/wiki/Metr%C3%B3_(Hungarian_band)" title="Metró (Hungarian band)">Metró</a> and <a href="/wiki/Omega_(band)" title="Omega (band)">Omega</a>, remain very popular, especially Omega, which has followings in Germany and beyond as well as in Hungary. Older veteran underground bands such as <a href="/wiki/Beatrice_(band)" title="Beatrice (band)">Beatrice</a>, from the 1980s, also remain popular.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-More_citations_needed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This article <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Hungary" title="Special:EditPage/Hungary">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Hungary%22">"Hungary"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Hungary%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Hungary%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Hungary%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Hungary%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Hungary%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2022</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_literature" title="Hungarian literature">Hungarian literature</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg/390px-Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg.png" decoding="async" width="390" height="99" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg/585px-Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg/780px-Szekely_Hungarian_Rovas_alphabet_Szekely_magyar_rovas_ABC.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1028" data-file-height="260" /></a><figcaption>The alphabet of the <a href="/wiki/Old_Hungarian_script" title="Old Hungarian script">Székely-Hungarian runiform</a>. The country switched to the Latin alphabet during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Stephen_I_of_Hungary" title="Stephen I of Hungary">King Saint Stephen</a> (1000–1038).</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the earliest times, Hungarian language was written in a <a href="/wiki/Old_Hungarian_script" title="Old Hungarian script">runic-like script</a> (although it was not used for literature purposes in the modern interpretation). The country switched to the <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Latin alphabet</a> after being Christianised under the reign of Stephen I of Hungary in the 11th century. The oldest remained written record in Hungarian language is a fragment in the <a href="/wiki/Establishing_charter_of_the_abbey_of_Tihany" title="Establishing charter of the abbey of Tihany">Establishing charter of the abbey of Tihany</a> (1055) which contains several Hungarian terms, among them the words <i>feheruuaru rea meneh hodu utu rea</i>, "up the military road to <a href="/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kesfeh%C3%A9rv%C3%A1r" title="Székesfehérvár">Fehérvár</a>" The rest of the document was written in Latin.<br />The oldest remaining complete text in Hungarian language is the <a href="/wiki/Funeral_Sermon_and_Prayer" title="Funeral Sermon and Prayer">Funeral Sermon and Prayer</a> <i>(Halotti beszéd és könyörgés)</i> (1192–1195), a translation of a Latin sermon. The oldest remaining poem in Hungarian is the Old Hungarian <i><a href="/wiki/Lamentations_of_Mary" title="Lamentations of Mary">Lamentations of Mary</a></i> <i>(Ómagyar Mária-siralom)</i>, also a (not very strict) translation from Latin, from the 13th century. It is also the oldest surviving <a href="/wiki/Uralic_languages" title="Uralic languages">Uralic</a> poem. Among the first chronicles about Hungarian history were <i><a href="/wiki/Gesta_Hungarorum" title="Gesta Hungarorum">Gesta Hungarorum</a></i> (<i>Deeds of the Hungarians</i>) by the unknown author usually called <a href="/wiki/Anonymus_(notary_of_B%C3%A9la_III)" title="Anonymus (notary of Béla III)">Anonymus</a>, and <i><a href="/wiki/Gesta_Hunnorum_et_Hungarorum" title="Gesta Hunnorum et Hungarorum">Gesta Hunnorum et Hungarorum</a></i> (<i>Deeds of the Huns and the Hungarians</i>) by Simon Kézai. Both are in Latin. These chronicles mix history with legends, so historically they are not always authentic. Another chronicle is the <i>Képes krónika</i> (<i>Illustrated Chronicle</i>), which was written for <a href="/wiki/Louis_I_of_Hungary" title="Louis I of Hungary">Louis the Great</a>.
</p>
<p><a href="/wiki/Renaissance_literature" title="Renaissance literature">Renaissance literature</a> flourished under the reign of King Matthias. <a href="/wiki/Janus_Pannonius" title="Janus Pannonius">Janus Pannonius</a>, although he wrote in Latin, is considered one of the most important persons in Hungarian literature, being the only significant Hungarian Humanist poet of the period. The first printing house was also founded during Matthias' reign, by <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A1s_Hess" title="András Hess">András Hess</a> in Buda. The first book printed in Hungary was the <i><a href="/wiki/Chronica_Hungarorum" title="Chronica Hungarorum">Chronica Hungarorum</a></i>. The most important poets of the period were <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A1lint_Balassi" title="Bálint Balassi">Bálint Balassi</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Zr%C3%ADnyi" title="Miklós Zrínyi">Miklós Zrínyi</a>. Balassi's poetry shows medieval influences, his poems can be divided into three sections: love poems, war poems and religious poems. Zrínyi's most significant work, the epic <i><a href="/wiki/The_Siege_of_Sziget" title="The Siege of Sziget">Szigeti veszedelem</a></i> (<i>The</i> <i>Peril of Sziget</i>, written in 1648/49) is written in a fashion similar to the <i><a href="/wiki/Iliad" title="Iliad">Iliad</a></i> and recounts the heroic <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Szigetv%C3%A1r" title="Siege of Szigetvár">battle of Szigetvár</a>, where his great-grandfather died while defending the castle of Szigetvár. Among the religious literary works, the most important is the Bible translation by <a href="/wiki/G%C3%A1sp%C3%A1r_K%C3%A1rolyi" title="Gáspár Károlyi">Gáspár Károlyi</a> (the second <a href="/wiki/Bible_translations_into_Hungarian" title="Bible translations into Hungarian">Hungarian Bible translation</a> in history), the Protestant pastor of <a href="/wiki/G%C3%B6nc" title="Gönc">Gönc</a>, in 1590. The translation is called the <i>Bible of Vizsoly</i>, after the town where it was first published.</p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Petofi.gif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Petofi.gif/220px-Petofi.gif" decoding="async" width="220" height="289" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Petofi.gif/330px-Petofi.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Petofi.gif/440px-Petofi.gif 2x" data-file-width="675" data-file-height="888" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/S%C3%A1ndor_Pet%C5%91fi" title="Sándor Petőfi">Sándor Petőfi</a>, Hungarian poet and revolutionary</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Hungarian enlightenment took place about fifty years after the French <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment" title="Age of Enlightenment">Enlightenment</a>. The first enlightened writers were <a href="/wiki/Maria_Theresa" title="Maria Theresa">Maria Theresa</a>'s bodyguards (<a href="/wiki/Gy%C3%B6rgy_Bessenyei" title="György Bessenyei">György Bessenyei</a>, <a href="/wiki/J%C3%A1nos_Bats%C3%A1nyi" title="János Batsányi">János Batsányi</a> and others). The greatest poets of the time were <a href="/wiki/Mih%C3%A1ly_Csokonai" title="Mihály Csokonai">Mihály Csokonai</a> and <a href="/wiki/D%C3%A1niel_Berzsenyi" title="Dániel Berzsenyi">Dániel Berzsenyi</a>. The greatest figure of the language reform was <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Kazinczy" title="Ferenc Kazinczy">Ferenc Kazinczy</a>. The Hungarian language became feasible for all type of scientific explanations from this time, and furthermore, many new words were coined for describing new inventions.
</p><p>Hungarian literature has recently gained some renown outside the borders of Hungary (mostly through translations into German, French and English). Some modern Hungarian authors have become increasingly popular in Germany and Italy especially <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A1ndor_M%C3%A1rai" title="Sándor Márai">Sándor Márai</a>, <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A9ter_Esterh%C3%A1zy" title="Péter Esterházy">Péter Esterházy</a>, <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A9ter_N%C3%A1das" title="Péter Nádas">Péter Nádas</a> and <a href="/wiki/Imre_Kert%C3%A9sz" title="Imre Kertész">Imre Kertész</a>. The latter is a contemporary Jewish writer who survived the Holocaust and won the Nobel Prize for literature in 2002. The older classics of Hungarian literature and Hungarian poetry have remained almost totally unknown outside Hungary. <a href="/wiki/J%C3%A1nos_Arany" title="János Arany">János Arany</a>, a famous 19th-century Hungarian poet, is still much loved in Hungary (especially his collection of ballads), among several other "true classics" like <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A1ndor_Pet%C5%91fi" title="Sándor Petőfi">Sándor Petőfi</a>, the poet of the Revolution of 1848, <a href="/wiki/Endre_Ady" title="Endre Ady">Endre Ady</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mih%C3%A1ly_Babits" title="Mihály Babits">Mihály Babits</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dezs%C5%91_Kosztol%C3%A1nyi" title="Dezső Kosztolányi">Dezső Kosztolányi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Attila_J%C3%B3zsef" title="Attila József">Attila József</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Radn%C3%B3ti" title="Miklós Radnóti">Miklós Radnóti</a> and <a href="/wiki/J%C3%A1nos_Pilinszky" title="János Pilinszky">János Pilinszky</a>. Other well-known Hungarian authors are <a href="/wiki/M%C3%B3r_J%C3%B3kai" title="Mór Jókai">Mór Jókai</a>. <a href="/wiki/Frigyes_Karinthy" title="Frigyes Karinthy">Frigyes Karinthy</a>, <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_Krasznahorkai" title="László Krasznahorkai">László Krasznahorkai</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_M%C3%B3ra" title="Ferenc Móra">Ferenc Móra</a>, <a href="/wiki/G%C3%A9za_G%C3%A1rdonyi" title="Géza Gárdonyi">Géza Gárdonyi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zsigmond_M%C3%B3ricz" title="Zsigmond Móricz">Zsigmond Móricz</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ephraim_Kishon" title="Ephraim Kishon">Ephraim Kishon</a>, <a href="/wiki/G%C3%A9za_G%C3%A1rdonyi" title="Géza Gárdonyi">Géza Gárdonyi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Koestler" title="Arthur Koestler">Arthur Koestler</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Moln%C3%A1r" title="Ferenc Molnár">Ferenc Molnár</a>, <a href="/wiki/Elie_Wiesel" title="Elie Wiesel">Elie Wiesel</a>, <a href="/wiki/K%C3%A1lm%C3%A1n_Miksz%C3%A1th" title="Kálmán Mikszáth">Kálmán Mikszáth</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gyula_Illy%C3%A9s" title="Gyula Illyés">Gyula Illyés</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Szentkuthy" title="Miklós Szentkuthy">Miklós Szentkuthy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Magda_Szab%C3%B3" title="Magda Szabó">Magda Szabó</a> and <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Vizinczey" title="Stephen Vizinczey">Stephen Vizinczey</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_cuisine" title="Hungarian cuisine">Hungarian cuisine</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_wine" title="Hungarian wine">Hungarian wine</a> and <a href="/wiki/Beer_in_Hungary" title="Beer in Hungary">Beer in Hungary</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG/220px-Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG/330px-Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG/440px-Hortobagy_palacsinta.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Hortob%C3%A1gyi_palacsinta" title="Hortobágyi palacsinta">Hortobágyi palacsinta</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sopron" title="Sopron">Sopron</a></figcaption></figure>
 <figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dobos_cake_(Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Dobos_cake_%28Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary%29.jpg/220px-Dobos_cake_%28Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Dobos_cake_%28Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary%29.jpg/330px-Dobos_cake_%28Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Dobos_cake_%28Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary%29.jpg/440px-Dobos_cake_%28Gerbeaud_Confectionery_Budapest_Hungary%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="853" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Dobos_torte" title="Dobos torte">Dobos torte</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Traditional dishes such as the world-famous <a href="/wiki/Goulash" title="Goulash">goulash</a> (<i>gulyás</i> stew or <i>gulyás</i> soup) feature prominently in Hungarian cuisine. Dishes are often flavoured with <a href="/wiki/Paprika" title="Paprika">paprika</a> (ground red peppers), a Hungarian innovation.<sup id="cite_ref-245" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-245">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> The paprika powder, obtained from a special type of pepper, is one of the most common spices used in typical Hungarian cuisine. Thick, heavy sour cream called <i><a href="/wiki/Tejf%C3%B6l" class="mw-redirect" title="Tejföl">tejföl</a></i> is often used to soften the flavour of a dish. The famous Hungarian hot river fish soup called <a href="/wiki/Fisherman%27s_soup" title="Fisherman&#39;s soup">fisherman's soup</a> or <i>halászlé</i> is usually a rich mixture of several kinds of poached fish.<sup id="cite_ref-246" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-246">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other dishes are <a href="/wiki/Chicken_paprikash" title="Chicken paprikash">chicken paprikash</a>, <a href="/wiki/Foie_gras" title="Foie gras">foie gras</a> made of goose liver, <i><a href="/wiki/P%C3%B6rk%C3%B6lt" title="Pörkölt">pörkölt</a></i> stew, <i>vadas</i>, (game stew with vegetable gravy and <a href="/wiki/Sp%C3%A4tzle" title="Spätzle">dumplings</a>), trout with almonds and salty and sweet dumplings, like <i><a href="/wiki/T%C3%BAr%C3%B3s_csusza" title="Túrós csusza">túrós csusza</a></i>, (dumplings with fresh <a href="/wiki/Quark_(dairy_product)" title="Quark (dairy product)">quark</a> cheese and thick sour cream). Desserts include the iconic <a href="/wiki/Dobos_torte" title="Dobos torte">Dobos torte</a>, <a href="/wiki/Strudel" title="Strudel">strudels</a> (<i>rétes</i>), filled with apple, cherry, poppy seed or cheese, <a href="/wiki/Palatschinke" title="Palatschinke">Gundel pancake</a>, plum dumplings (<i><a href="/wiki/Kn%C3%B6del" title="Knödel">szilvás gombóc</a></i>), <i>somlói</i> dumplings, dessert soups like chilled <a href="/wiki/Sour_cherry_soup" title="Sour cherry soup">sour cherry soup</a> and sweet chestnut puree, <i>gesztenyepüré</i> (cooked <a href="/wiki/Chestnut" title="Chestnut">chestnuts</a> mashed with sugar and rum and split into crumbs, topped with whipped cream). <i><a href="/wiki/Pretzel" title="Pretzel">Perec</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Kifli" title="Kifli">kifli</a></i> are widely popular pastries.<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>csárda</i> is the most distinctive type of Hungarian inn, an old-style tavern offering traditional cuisine and beverages. <i>Borozó</i> usually denotes a cosy old-fashioned wine tavern, <i>pince</i> is a beer or wine cellar and a <i>söröző</i> is a <a href="/wiki/Pub" title="Pub">pub</a> offering draught beer and sometimes meals. The <i>bisztró</i> is an inexpensive restaurant often with self-service. The <i>büfé</i> is the cheapest place, although one may have to eat standing at a counter. Pastries, cakes and coffee are served at the confectionery called <i>cukrászda</i>, while an <i>eszpresszó</i> is a café.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg/170px-Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg/255px-Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg/340px-Tokaji_6p_1989.jpg 2x" data-file-width="880" data-file-height="1320" /></a><figcaption>The famous <a href="/wiki/Tokaji" title="Tokaji">Tokaji</a> wine. It was called <i>Vinum Regum, Rex Vinorum</i> ("Wine of Kings, King of Wines") by <a href="/wiki/Louis_XIV" title="Louis XIV">Louis XIV of France</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/P%C3%A1linka" title="Pálinka">Pálinka</a> is a fruit brandy, distilled from fruit grown in the orchards situated on the Great Hungarian Plain. It is a spirit native to Hungary and comes in a variety of flavours including apricot (<i>barack</i>) and cherry (<i>cseresznye</i>). However, plum (<i>szilva</i>) is the most popular flavour. Beer goes well with many traditional Hungarian dishes. The five main Hungarian beer brands are: <a href="/wiki/Borsod_Brewery" title="Borsod Brewery">Borsodi</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Soproni&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Soproni (page does not exist)">Soproni</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Arany_%C3%81szok&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Arany Ászok (page does not exist)">Arany Ászok</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=K%C3%B5b%C3%A1nyai&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Kõbányai (page does not exist)">Kõbányai</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Dreher_Brewery" class="mw-redirect" title="Dreher Brewery">Dreher</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-248" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-248">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> People traditionally do not clink their glasses or mugs when drinking beer. There is an urban legend in Hungarian culture that Austrian generals clinked their beer glasses to celebrate the execution of <a href="/wiki/The_13_Martyrs_of_Arad" title="The 13 Martyrs of Arad">the 13 Martyrs of Arad</a> in 1849. Many people still follow the tradition, although younger people often disavow it, citing that the vow was only meant to last 150 years.<sup id="cite_ref-249" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-249">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Hungarian_wine" title="Hungarian wine">Hungary is ideal for wine-making</a>, and the country can be divided into numerous regions.<sup id="cite_ref-250" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-250">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> The Romans brought vines to Pannonia, and by the 5th century AD, there are records of extensive vineyards in what is now Hungary. The Hungarians brought their wine-making knowledge from the East. According to <a href="/wiki/Ahmad_ibn_Rustah" title="Ahmad ibn Rustah">Ibn Rustah</a>, the Hungarian tribes were familiar with wine-making long before their <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_conquest_of_the_Carpathian_Basin" title="Hungarian conquest of the Carpathian Basin">conquest of the Carpathian Basin</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> The different wine regions offer a great variety of styles: the main products of the country are elegant and full-bodied dry whites with good acidity, although complex sweet whites (<a href="/wiki/Tokaj_wine_region" title="Tokaj wine region">Tokaj</a>), elegant (<a href="/wiki/Eger_wine_region" title="Eger wine region">Eger</a>) and full-bodied robust reds (<a href="/wiki/Vill%C3%A1ny" title="Villány">Villány</a> and <a href="/wiki/Szeksz%C3%A1rd" title="Szekszárd">Szekszárd</a>). The main varieties are: Olaszrizling, <a href="/wiki/H%C3%A1rslevel%C5%B1" title="Hárslevelű">Hárslevelű</a>, <a href="/wiki/Furmint" title="Furmint">Furmint</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pinot_gris" title="Pinot gris">Pinot gris</a> or Szürkebarát, <a href="/wiki/Chardonnay" title="Chardonnay">Chardonnay</a> (whites), Kékfrankos (or <a href="/wiki/Blaufr%C3%A4nkisch" title="Blaufränkisch">Blaufrankisch</a> in German), <a href="/wiki/Kadarka" title="Kadarka">Kadarka</a>, <a href="/wiki/Blauer_Portugieser" title="Blauer Portugieser">Portugieser</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zweigelt" title="Zweigelt">Zweigelt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cabernet_Sauvignon" title="Cabernet Sauvignon">Cabernet Sauvignon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cabernet_Franc" title="Cabernet Franc">Cabernet Franc</a> and <a href="/wiki/Merlot" title="Merlot">Merlot</a>. The most famous wines from Hungary are <a href="/wiki/Tokaji" title="Tokaji">Tokaji Aszú</a> and <a href="/wiki/Egri_Bikav%C3%A9r" title="Egri Bikavér">Egri Bikavér</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Tokaji" title="Tokaji">Tokaji</a>, meaning "of Tokaj", or "from Tokaj" in Hungarian, is used to label wines from the wine region of Tokaj. Tokaji wine has received accolades from numerous great writers and composers including <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_van_Beethoven" title="Ludwig van Beethoven">Beethoven</a>, <a href="/wiki/Franz_Liszt" title="Franz Liszt">Liszt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Franz_Schubert" title="Franz Schubert">Schubert</a> and <a href="/wiki/Johann_Wolfgang_von_Goethe" title="Johann Wolfgang von Goethe">Goethe</a>; <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Haydn" title="Joseph Haydn">Joseph Haydn</a>'s favourite wine was a Tokaji.<sup id="cite_ref-royal_tokaji_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-royal_tokaji-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Louis_XV" title="Louis XV">Louis XV</a> and <a href="/wiki/Frederick_the_Great" title="Frederick the Great">Frederick the Great</a> tried to outdo one another when they entertained guests with Tokaji. <a href="/wiki/Napoleon_III" title="Napoleon III">Napoleon III</a> ordered 30–40 barrels of Tokaji at the French Royal Court every year. <a href="/wiki/Gustav_III" title="Gustav III">Gustav III</a> of Sweden loved Tokaji.<sup id="cite_ref-royal_tokaji_254-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-royal_tokaji-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> In Russia, customers included <a href="/wiki/Peter_the_Great" title="Peter the Great">Peter the Great</a> and <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_of_Russia" title="Elizabeth of Russia">Empress Elizabeth</a>, while <a href="/wiki/Catherine_the_Great" title="Catherine the Great">Catherine the Great</a> actually established a Russian garrison in the town of Tokaj with the aim of assuring regular wine deliveries to <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Saint Petersburg</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-royal_tokaji_254-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-royal_tokaji-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For over 150 years, a blend of forty Hungarian herbs has been used to create the liqueur <a href="/wiki/Unicum" title="Unicum">unicum</a>. Unicum is a bitter, dark-coloured liqueur that can be drunk as an apéritif or after a meal, thus helping digestion.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Folk_art">Folk art</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg/220px-Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg/330px-Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg/440px-Voivodina_Hungarians_national_costume_and_dance_6.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1302" data-file-height="1340" /></a><figcaption>Hungarians in traditional garments / folk costumes dancing the <a href="/wiki/Cs%C3%A1rd%C3%A1s" title="Csárdás">csárdás</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Ugr%C3%B3s" title="Ugrós">Ugrós</a> (jumping dances) are old-style dances dating back to the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>. The ugrós can include solo or couple dances accompanied by old-style music, shepherd and other solo man's dances from Transylvania, and marching dances, along with remnants of medieval weapon dances. <a href="/wiki/Karik%C3%A1z%C3%B3" title="Karikázó">Karikázó</a> is a circle dance performed by women accompanied by the singing of folk songs. <a href="/wiki/Cs%C3%A1rd%C3%A1s" title="Csárdás">Csárdás</a> are newer style dances developed in the 18 and 19th centuries, which includes embroidered costumes and energetic music. From the men's intricate boot slapping dances to the ancient women's circle dances, Csárdás demonstrates the infectious exuberance of the Hungarian folk dancing still celebrated in the villages. <a href="/wiki/Verbunkos" title="Verbunkos">Verbunkos</a> is a solo man's dance evolved from the recruiting performances of the Austro-Hungarian army. The <a href="/wiki/Leg%C3%A9nyes" title="Legényes">legényes</a> is a men's solo dance done by the ethnic Hungarian people living in the <a href="/wiki/Kalotaszeg" class="mw-redirect" title="Kalotaszeg">Kalotaszeg</a> region of Transylvania. Although usually danced by young men, it can be also danced by older men. The dance is generally performed freestyle by one dancer at a time in front of a band. Women participate in the dance by standing in lines to the side and singing or shouting verses while the men dance. Each man performs a number of points (dance phrases), typically four to eight without repetition. Each point consists of four parts, each lasting four counts. The first part is usually the same for everyone (there are only a few variations).
</p><p>It was in the beginning of the 18th-century that the present style of Hungarian folk art took shape, incorporating both <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a> and <a href="/wiki/Baroque" title="Baroque">Baroque</a> elements, depending on the area, as well as Persian <a href="/wiki/Sasanian_Empire" title="Sasanian Empire">Sassanid</a> influences. Flowers and leaves, sometimes a bird or a spiral ornament, are the principal decorative themes. The most frequent ornament is a flower with a centrepiece resembling the eye of a peacock's feather. Nearly all the manifestations of folk art practiced elsewhere in Europe also flourished among the Magyar peasantry at one time or another, their ceramics and textile being the most highly developed of all. The finest achievements in their textile arts are the embroideries which vary from region to region. Those of Kalotaszeg are charming products of Oriental design, sewn chiefly in a single colour—red, blue, or black. Soft in line, the embroideries are applied on altar cloths, pillowcases, and sheets.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Tolna_County" title="Tolna County">Sárköz</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mez%C5%91k%C3%B6vesd" title="Mezőkövesd">Matyóföld</a> regions produce the finest embroideries. The women's caps generally exhibit black and white designs as delicate as lace and give evidence of the people's wonderfully subtle artistic feeling. The embroidery motifs applied to women's wear have also been transposed to tablecloths and runners suitable for modern use as wall decorations.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sport">Sport</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg/220px-Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg/330px-Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg/440px-Vaterpolo_Hungary_vs_Italy_semifinal_game2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1936" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Hungary_men%27s_national_water_polo_team" title="Hungary men&#39;s national water polo team">Hungary men's national water polo team</a> is considered among the best in the world, holding the world record for Olympic golds and overall medals.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Hungary_at_the_Olympics" title="Hungary at the Olympics">Hungarian athletes</a> have been successful contenders in the <a href="/wiki/Summer_Olympic_Games" title="Summer Olympic Games">Summer Olympic Games</a>. Hungary ranks 9th with a total of 511 medals in the <a href="/wiki/All-time_Olympic_Games_medal_table" title="All-time Olympic Games medal table">all-time Summer Olympic Games medal count</a>. Hungary has the third-highest number of <a href="/wiki/Olympic_medal" title="Olympic medal">Olympic medals</a> per capita and second-highest number of gold medals per capita in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> Hungary has historically excelled in Olympic water sports. In <a href="/wiki/Water_polo" title="Water polo">water polo</a> the men's Hungarian team is the <a href="/wiki/Water_polo_at_the_Summer_Olympics" title="Water polo at the Summer Olympics">leading medal winner by a significant margin</a>, and in swimming <a href="/wiki/List_of_Olympic_medalists_in_swimming_(men)" title="List of Olympic medalists in swimming (men)">the men's</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_Olympic_medalists_in_swimming_(women)" title="List of Olympic medalists in swimming (women)">the women's</a> teams are both rank fifth-most successful. Hungary leads the overall medal count in <a href="/wiki/Canoeing_at_the_Summer_Olympics" title="Canoeing at the Summer Olympics">canoeing and kayaking</a>. Hungary won its first gold medal in Winter Olympics in 2018 in men's short track speed skating with a team of four: <a href="/wiki/Csaba_Burj%C3%A1n" title="Csaba Burján">Csaba Burján</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shaolin_S%C3%A1ndor_Liu" title="Shaolin Sándor Liu">Shaolin Sándor Liu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shaoang_Liu" title="Shaoang Liu">Shaoang Liu</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Viktor_Knoch" title="Viktor Knoch">Viktor Knoch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Groupama_Arena_Budapest_(17267881892).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Groupama_Arena_Budapest_%2817267881892%29.jpg/220px-Groupama_Arena_Budapest_%2817267881892%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Groupama_Arena_Budapest_%2817267881892%29.jpg/330px-Groupama_Arena_Budapest_%2817267881892%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Groupama_Arena_Budapest_%2817267881892%29.jpg/440px-Groupama_Arena_Budapest_%2817267881892%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5465" data-file-height="3644" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Ferencv%C3%A1ros_Stadion" title="Ferencváros Stadion">Groupama Aréna</a>, home of&#160;<a href="/wiki/Ferencv%C3%A1rosi_TC" title="Ferencvárosi TC">Ferencvárosi TC</a>, a <a href="/wiki/UEFA_stadium_categories" title="UEFA stadium categories">UEFA Category 4 Stadium</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Hungary hosted many global sports events, including the <a href="/wiki/1997_World_Amateur_Boxing_Championships" title="1997 World Amateur Boxing Championships">1997 World Amateur Boxing Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2000_World_Fencing_Championships" title="2000 World Fencing Championships">2000 World Fencing Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2001_World_Allround_Speed_Skating_Championships" title="2001 World Allround Speed Skating Championships">2001 World Allround Speed Skating Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2008_World_Interuniversity_Games" title="2008 World Interuniversity Games">2008 World Interuniversity Games</a>, <a href="/wiki/2008_World_Modern_Pentathlon_Championships" title="2008 World Modern Pentathlon Championships">2008 World Modern Pentathlon Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2010_ITU_World_Championship_Series" title="2010 ITU World Championship Series">2010 ITU World Championship Series</a>, 2011 <a href="/wiki/IIHF_World_Championship" class="mw-redirect" title="IIHF World Championship">IIHF World Championship</a>, <a href="/wiki/2013_World_Fencing_Championships" title="2013 World Fencing Championships">2013 World Fencing Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2013_World_Wrestling_Championships" title="2013 World Wrestling Championships">2013 World Wrestling Championships</a>, 2014 <a href="/wiki/World_Masters_Athletics_Championships" title="World Masters Athletics Championships">World Masters Athletics Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2017_World_Aquatics_Championships" title="2017 World Aquatics Championships">2017 World Aquatics Championships</a> and <a href="/wiki/2017_World_Judo_Championships" title="2017 World Judo Championships">2017 World Judo Championships</a>, only in the last two decade. Besides these, Hungary was the home of many European-level tournaments, like <a href="/wiki/2006_European_Aquatics_Championships" title="2006 European Aquatics Championships">2006 European Aquatics Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2010_European_Aquatics_Championships" title="2010 European Aquatics Championships">2010 European Aquatics Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2013_European_Judo_Championships" title="2013 European Judo Championships">2013 European Judo Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2013_European_Karate_Championships" title="2013 European Karate Championships">2013 European Karate Championships</a>, <a href="/wiki/2017_European_Rhythmic_Gymnastics_Championships" class="mw-redirect" title="2017 European Rhythmic Gymnastics Championships">2017 European Rhythmic Gymnastics Championship</a> and hosted 4 matches in the <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2020" title="UEFA Euro 2020">UEFA Euro 2020</a>, which were held in the 67,889-seat new <a href="/wiki/Multi-purpose_stadium" title="Multi-purpose stadium">multi-purpose</a> <a href="/wiki/New_Pusk%C3%A1s_Ferenc_Stadium" class="mw-redirect" title="New Puskás Ferenc Stadium">Puskás Ferenc Stadium</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_Grand_Prix" title="Hungarian Grand Prix">Hungarian Grand Prix</a> in <a href="/wiki/Formula_One" title="Formula One">Formula One</a> has been held at the <a href="/wiki/Hungaroring" title="Hungaroring">Hungaroring</a> just outside Budapest, which circuit has <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A9d%C3%A9ration_Internationale_de_l%27Automobile" title="Fédération Internationale de l&#39;Automobile">FIA</a> Grade 1 license.<sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> Since 1986, the race has been a round of the <a href="/wiki/Formula_One" title="Formula One">Formula One</a> World Championship. The track was completely resurfaced for the first time in early 2016, and it was announced the Grand Prix's deal was extended for a further five years, until 2026.<sup id="cite_ref-259" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-259">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Chess" title="Chess">Chess</a> is a popular and successful sport, and the Hungarian players are the eighth most powerful overall on the ranking of <a href="/wiki/FIDE" title="FIDE">World Chess Federation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> There are about 54 <a href="/wiki/Grandmaster_(chess)" title="Grandmaster (chess)">Grandmasters</a> and 118 <a href="/wiki/FIDE_titles" title="FIDE titles">International Masters</a>, which is more than in <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> or <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom" title="United Kingdom">United Kingdom</a>. <a href="/wiki/Judit_Polg%C3%A1r" title="Judit Polgár">Judit Polgár</a> generally considered the <a href="/wiki/Glossary_of_chess#Strength" title="Glossary of chess">strongest</a> female chess player of all time. Some of the world's best <a href="/wiki/Sabre_(fencing)" title="Sabre (fencing)">sabre</a> athletes have historically also hailed from Hungary,<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> and in 2009, the <a href="/wiki/Hungary_men%27s_national_ice_hockey_team" title="Hungary men&#39;s national ice hockey team">Hungary men's national ice hockey team</a> qualified for their first <a href="/wiki/Ice_Hockey_World_Championships" title="Ice Hockey World Championships">IIHF World Championship</a>, in 2015, they qualified for their second world championship in the top division.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Football">Football</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Football_in_Hungary" title="Football in Hungary">Football in Hungary</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg/170px-Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="259" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg/255px-Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg/340px-Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2399" data-file-height="3650" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s" title="Ferenc Puskás">Ferenc Puskás</a>, the greatest top division scorer of the 20th century. The <a href="/wiki/FIFA_Pusk%C3%A1s_Award" title="FIFA Puskás Award">FIFA Puskás Award</a> is named in his honour.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Hungary has won three Olympic football titles, and the country finished as runners-up in the <a href="/wiki/1938_FIFA_World_Cup" title="1938 FIFA World Cup">1938</a> and <a href="/wiki/1954_FIFA_World_Cup" title="1954 FIFA World Cup">1954 FIFA World Cups</a>, and third in <a href="/wiki/1964_European_Nations%27_Cup" title="1964 European Nations&#39; Cup">Euro 1964</a>. Hungary revolutionised the sport in the 1950s, laying the tactical fundamentals of <a href="/wiki/Total_Football" title="Total Football">total football</a> and dominating international football with the <i>Aranycsapat</i> ("<a href="/wiki/Golden_Team" title="Golden Team">Golden Team</a>"), which included <a href="/wiki/Ferenc_Pusk%C3%A1s" title="Ferenc Puskás">Ferenc Puskás</a>, top goal scorer of the 20th century,<sup id="cite_ref-FIFA_benefit_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FIFA_benefit-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Spanish_obit_264-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Spanish_obit-264">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Puskás_auction_265-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Puskás_auction-265">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> to whom FIFA dedicated<sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> its newest award, the <a href="/wiki/FIFA_Pusk%C3%A1s_Award" title="FIFA Puskás Award">Puskás Award</a>. The team of that era has the second <a href="/wiki/World_Football_Elo_Ratings#All-time_highest_ratings" title="World Football Elo Ratings">all-time highest Football Elo Rating in the world</a>, with 2166, and one of the longest undefeated runs in football history, remaining unbeaten in 31 games spanning more than four years.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The post-golden age decades saw a gradually weakening Hungary, though recently there is renewal in all aspects. The Hungarian Children's Football Federation was founded in 2008, as youth development thrives. They hosted the <a href="/wiki/2010_UEFA_Futsal_Championship" title="2010 UEFA Futsal Championship">2010 UEFA Futsal Championship</a> in Budapest and Debrecen, the first time the <a href="/wiki/Hungarian_Football_Federation" title="Hungarian Football Federation">MLSZ</a> staged a <a href="/wiki/UEFA" title="UEFA">UEFA</a> finals tournament. Also, the national teams have produced some surprise successes such as beating <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2004" title="UEFA Euro 2004">Euro 2004</a> winner <a href="/wiki/Greece_national_football_team" title="Greece national football team">Greece</a> 3–2<sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/2006_FIFA_World_Cup" title="2006 FIFA World Cup">2006 FIFA World Cup</a> winner <a href="/wiki/Italy_national_football_team" title="Italy national football team">Italy</a> 3–1.<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> During <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2016" title="UEFA Euro 2016">UEFA Euro 2016</a> Hungary won <a href="/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2016_Group_F" title="UEFA Euro 2016 Group F">Group F</a> and were eventually defeated in the round of 16.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Media">Media</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-Expand_section plainlinks metadata ambox mbox-small-left ambox-content" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="[icon]" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/20px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png" decoding="async" width="20" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/30px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/40px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="44" data-file-height="31" /></a></span></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs expansion</b>. You can help by <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Hungary&amp;action=edit&amp;section=">adding to it</a>.  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">August 2023</span>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mass_media_in_Hungary" title="Mass media in Hungary">Mass media in Hungary</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Television_in_Hungary" title="Television in Hungary">Television in Hungary</a>, <a href="/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Hungary" title="List of newspapers in Hungary">List of newspapers in Hungary</a>, and <a href="/wiki/List_of_magazines_in_Hungary" title="List of magazines in Hungary">List of magazines in Hungary</a></div>
<p>During the Communist era (1945–1989) the mass media in Hungary were mainly under <a href="/wiki/Stalinism" title="Stalinism">Stalinist</a> control.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup> In the 2020s major television channels include <a href="/wiki/TV2_(Hungarian_TV_channel)" title="TV2 (Hungarian TV channel)">TV2</a> and <a href="/wiki/RTL_(Hungarian_TV_channel)" title="RTL (Hungarian TV channel)">RTL</a> and radio networks include Karc FM and Radio 1. Some daily newspapers, such as <i><a href="/wiki/Magyar_H%C3%ADrlap" title="Magyar Hírlap">Magyar Hírlap</a></i>, no longer issue print versions but maintain just an online presence, and others have closed in recent years. Many media outlets have "merged to form a conglomerate", the <a href="/wiki/Central_European_Press_and_Media_Foundation" title="Central European Press and Media Foundation">Central European Press and Media Foundation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-97"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-97">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFBernstein2003" class="citation news cs1">Bernstein, Richard (9 August 2003). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://query.nytimes.com/gst/fullpage.html?res=9B07E3D91531F93AA3575BC0A9659C8B63&amp;pagewanted=2">"East on the Danube: Hungary's Tragic Century"</a>. <i>The New York Times</i><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">15 March</span> 2008</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=The+New+York+Times&amp;rft.atitle=East+on+the+Danube%3A+Hungary%27s+Tragic+Century&amp;rft.date=2003-08-09&amp;rft.aulast=Bernstein&amp;rft.aufirst=Richard&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fquery.nytimes.com%2Fgst%2Ffullpage.html%3Fres%3D9B07E3D91531F93AA3575BC0A9659C8B63%26pagewanted%3D2&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AHungary" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-J._Lee_Ready_1995_page_130-98"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-J._Lee_Ready_1995_page_130_98-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-J._Lee_Ready_1995_page_130_98-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">J. Lee Ready (1995), <i>World War Two. Nation by Nation</i>, London, Cassell, page 130. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/1-85409-290-1" title="Special:BookSources/1-85409-290-1">1-85409-290-1</a></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-bbc-no-warning-to-hungary-jews-99"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-bbc-no-warning-to-hungary-jews_99-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFMike_Thomson2012" class="citation web cs1">Mike Thomson (13 November 2012). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/world-europe-20267659">"Could the BBC have done more to help Hungarian Jews?"</a>. BBC (British broadcasting service). <q>the BBC broadcast every day, giving updates on the war, general news and opinion pieces on Hungarian politics. But among all these broadcasts, there were crucial things that were not being said, things that might have warned thousands of Hungarian Jews of the horrors to come in the event of German occupation. A memo setting out policy for the BBC Hungarian Service in 1942 states: "We shouldn't mention the Jews at all". By 1943, the BBC Polish Service was broadcasting the exterminations. And yet his policy of silence on the Jews was followed until the German invasion in March 1944. After the tanks rolled in, the Hungarian Service did then broadcast warnings. But by then it was too late "Many Hungarian Jews who survived the deportations claimed that they had not been informed by their leaders, that no one had told them. But there's plenty of evidence that they could have known," said David Cesarani, professor of history at Royal Holloway, University of London.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=Could+the+BBC+have+done+more+to+help+Hungarian+Jews%3F&amp;rft.pub=BBC+%28British+broadcasting+service%29&amp;rft.date=2012-11-13&amp;rft.au=Mike+Thomson&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.bbc.co.uk%2Fnews%2Fworld-europe-20267659&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AHungary" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-239"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-239">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20101214123942/http://budapestcorner.com/index.php/flats/general-information">"General information on various student flats and building types in Budapest"</a>. </li>
<li id="cite_note-240"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-240">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://mek.oszk.hu/02100/02172/html/index.html">Szabolcsi</a> Although the Hungarian upper class has long had cultural and political connections with the rest of Europe, leading to an influx of European musical ideas, the rural peasants maintained their own traditions such that by the end of the 19th century Hungarian composers could draw on rural peasant music to (re)create a Hungarian classical style. For example, <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9la_Bart%C3%B3k" title="Béla Bartók">Béla Bartók</a> and <a href="/wiki/Zolt%C3%A1n_Kod%C3%A1ly" title="Zoltán Kodály">Zoltán Kodály</a>, two of Hungary's most famous composers, are known for using folk themes in their own music.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-241"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-241">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Szalipszki, p. 12<br />Refers to the country as "widely considered" to be a "home of music".</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Broughton-242"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Broughton_242-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Broughton, pp. 159–167</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-243"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-243">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Szabolcsi, <i>The Specific Conditions of Hungarian Musical Development</i><br />"Every experiment, made from Hungarian antecedents and on Hungarian soil, to create a conscious musical culture (music written by composers, as different from folk music), had instinctively or consciously striven to develop widely and universally the musical world of the folk song. Folk poetry and folk music were deeply embedded in the collective Hungarian people's culture, and this unity did not cease to be effective even when it was given from and expression by individual creative artists, performers and poets."</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-252"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-252">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This is the world-famous sweet, topaz-colored wine known throughout the English-speaking world as <b>Tokay</b>. "A rich, sweet, moderately strong wine of a topaz color, produced in the vicinity of Tokay, in Hungary; also, a similar wine produced elsewhere." <i>Webster's New International Dictionary of the English Language</i> (Springfield, Mass.: G.&amp;C. Merriam, 1913). See <i>Tokay</i> at page 2166.</span>
